```python
                if x2 - x1 == y2 - y1:
                    t_val = x2 - x1
                    if t_val >= 0:
                        critical_times.append(t_val)
        
        # L and D: x1 - t = x2 and y1 - t = y2 => t = x1 - x2 = y1 - y2
        for x1, y1 in l_points:
            for x2, y2 in d_points:
                if x1 - x2 == y1 - y2:
                    t_val = x1 - x2
                    if t_val >= 0:
                        critical_times.append(t_val)
        
        # Also need to consider when the entire set has the same x or same y
        # but I think the above covers most cases.
        
        # Remove duplicates and sort
        critical_times = sorted(set(critical_times))
        
        # Also add common times if we have any
        # and we need to check t=0 already
        
        # Now evaluate the area at each critical time
        min_area = float('inf')
        
        for t in critical_times:
            area = get_area(t)
            if area < min_area:
                min_area = area
        
        # Also, in the sample, t=0 gives area = (3-0) * (3-0) = 9, but that's not minimum
        # At t=3, area=0
        
        # But what if no meeting is possible? We need to check if area can be 0.
        
        # We already have the area at critical times.
        
        print(min_area)
    
    else:
        # Cannot have all points meet at same point
        # Check critical times
        critical_times = [0]
        
        # Add meeting times from pairs of points in opposite directions
        
        # R and L
        r_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'R']
        l_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'L']
        
        for x1, y1 in r_points:
            for x2, y2 in l_points:
                if x2 > x1:
                    critical_times.append((x2 - x1) / 2)
        
        # U and D
        u_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'U']
        d_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'D']
        
        for x1, y1 in u_points:
            for x2, y2 in d_points:
                if y2 > y1:
                    critical_times.append((y2 - y1) / 2)
        
        # Only add unique non-negative times
        critical_times = sorted(set(t for t in critical_times if t >= 0))
        
        min_area = float('inf')
        for t in critical_times:
            area = get_area(t)
            if area < min_area:
                min_area = area
        
        print(min_area)

# This is still not robust. Let me implement a simpler but correct solution.

# After research, I recall that for this type of problem, the minimum area is achieved when the bounding box is minimized, and since the movement is linear, we can check the area at t=0 and at times when pairwise meetings occur.

# Given time, I'll implement a more reliable solution:

n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, y, d = input().split()
    points.append((int(x), int(y), d))

# If all points are already at same location
x_coords = [p[0] for p in points]
y_coords = [p[1] for p in points]
if len(set(x_coords)) == 1 and len(set(y_coords)) == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    def get_area(t):
        x_vals = []
        y_vals = []
        for x, y, d in points:
            if d == 'R':
                x_vals.append(x + t)
                y_vals.append(y)
            elif d == 'L':
                x_vals.append(x - t)
                y_vals.append(y)
            elif d == 'U':
                x_vals.append(x)
                y_vals.append(y + t)
            elif d == 'D':
                x_vals.append(x)
                y_vals.append(y - t)
        
        x_min = min(x_vals)
        x_max = max(x_vals)
        y_min = min(y_vals)
        y_max = max(y_vals)
        
        return (x_max - x_min) * (y_max - y_min)
    
    # Critical times: t=0, and times when pairs of points meet
    critical_times = [0.0]
    
    # R and L meet when x1 + t = x2 - t => t = (x2 - x1)/2
    r_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'R']
    l_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'L']
    for x1, y1 in r_points:
        for x2, y2 in l_points:
            t_val = (x2 - x1) / 2.0
            if t_val >= 0:
                critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # U and D meet when y1 + t = y2 - t => t = (y2 - y1)/2
    u_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'U']
    d_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'D']
    for x1, y1 in u_points:
        for x2, y2 in d_points:
            t_val = (y2 - y1) / 2.0
            if t_val >= 0:
                critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # Also consider diagonal meetings (e.g., R and D, etc.)
    r_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'R']
    d_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'D']
    for x1, y1 in r_points:
        for x2, y2 in d_points:
            # R: (x1, y1) -> (x1+t, y1)
            # D: (x2, y2) -> (x2, y2-t)
            # They meet if x1+t = x2 and y1 = y2-t => t = x2 - x1 and t = y2 - y1
            # So need x2 - x1 == y2 - y1
            if x2 - x1 == y2 - y1:
                t_val = x2 - x1
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # L and U
    l_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'L']
    u_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'U']
    for x1, y1 in l_points:
        for x2, y2 in u_points:
            # L: (x1, y1) -> (x1-t, y1)
            # U: (x2, y2) -> (x2, y2+t)
            # Meet if x1-t = x2 and y1 = y2+t => t = x1 - x2 and t = y1 - y2
            # Need x1 - x2 == y1 - y2
            if x1 - x2 == y1 - y2:
                t_val = x1 - x2
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # R and U
    r_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'R']
    u_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'U']
    for x1, y1 in r_points:
        for x2, y2 in u_points:
            # R: (x1, y1) -> (x1+t, y1)
            # U: (x2, y2) -> (x2, y2+t)
            # Meet if x1+t = x2 and y1 = y2+t => t = x2 - x1 and t = y2 - y1
            # Need x2 - x1 == y2 - y1
            if x2 - x1 == y2 - y1:
                t_val = x2 - x1
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # L and D
    l_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'L']
    d_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'D']
    for x1, y1 in l_points:
        for x2, y2 in d_points:
            # L: (x1, y1) -> (x1-t, y1)
            # D: (x2, y2) -> (x2, y2-t)
            # Meet if x1-t = x2 and y1 = y2-t => t = x1 - x2 and t = y1 - y2
            # Need x1 - x2 == y1 - y2
            if x1 - x2 == y1 - y2:
                t_val = x1 - x2
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # Remove duplicates and sort
    critical_times = sorted(set(critical_times))
    
    min_area = float('inf')
    for t in critical_times:
        area = get_area(t)
        if area < min_area:
            min_area = area
    
    print(min_area if min_area != float('inf') else 0)
```

Wait, there's a simpler insight: the area is always non-negative, and if there's any t that makes the area 0, we return 0.

Also, the function might have a minimum at some critical time.

But let me test with the sample:
- (0,3,D), (3,0,L)
- At t=0: area = (3-0) * (3-0) = 9
- At t=3: (0,3) becomes (0,0), (3,0) becomes (0,0), area = 0

So we need to include t=3 in our critical times.

In our diagonal meeting, for R and D, we have no points. For L and U: (3,0) and (0,3)
- x1 - x2 = 3 - 0 = 3, y1 - y2 = 0 - 3 = -3, not equal
- For L and D: we have (3,0) and (0,3): x1 - x2 = 3, y1 - y2 = -3, not equal
- For R and U: no points
- For R and D: no points

But we should have R and L: (0,3) has d='D', (3,0) has d='L'
So for R and L: we don't have any R points.

We need to include U and D meetings: (0,3) is U, (3,0) is L - only one U and one D? (0,3) is D.

So only one D and one L.

For U and D: only one each.

So our critical times from U and D: for (0,3) and no U, so no pair.

I see the issue: in the sample, the meeting is between L and D, not U and D.

The point (0,3) is D, so it moves to (0,3-t)
The point (3,0) is L, so it moves to (3-t,0)

They meet when 0 = 3-t and 3-t = 0, so t=3.

In our code, we didn't have a meeting between L and D in the pairs.

We need to include L and D meetings in our list.

In the code above, we have a section for L and D, but it's under the wrong condition.

Let me fix the L and D section:

For L and D: 
- L point (x1,y1) -> (x1-t, y1)
- D point (x2,y2) -> (x2, y2-t)
- They meet if x1-t = x2 and y1 = y2-t
- This gives: t = x1 - x2 and t = y1 - y2
- So need x1 - x2 = y1 - y2

In the sample: (3,0) and (0,3)
- x1=3, y1=0, x2=0, y2=3
- x1 - x2 = 3, y1 - y2 = -3, not equal.

But they meet at t=3: (3-3,0) = (0,0), (0,3-3) = (0,0)

So the meeting conditions are:
- x1 - t = x2 and y1 = y2 - t
- So t = x1 - x2 and t = y2 - y1
- So need x1 - x2 = y2 - y1

In the sample: x1 - x2 = 3 - 0 = 3, y2 - y1 = 3 - 0 = 3, so 3=3, yes.

So the condition should be x1 - x2 == y2 - y1.

I see the mistake in the code.

Let me fix the L and D section:

This should work for the sample: 
- L and D: (3,0) and (0,3): x1 - x2 = 3 - 0 = 3, y2 - y1 = 3 - 0 = 3, so 3=3, t_val = 3
- Then area at t=3 is 0.

I think this is correct.

Note: The pairwise meeting conditions might be sufficient because the area function is piecewise quadratic and the minimum must occur at one of the meeting times or at t=0.

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, y, d = input().split()
    points.append((int(x), int(y), d))

def get_area(t):
    x_vals = []
    y_vals = []
    for x, y, d in points:
        if d == 'R':
            x_vals.append(x + t)
            y_vals.append(y)
        elif d == 'L':
            x_vals.append(x - t)
            y_vals.append(y)
        elif d == 'U':
            x_vals.append(x)
            y_vals.append(y + t)
        elif d == 'D':
            x_vals.append(x)
            y_vals.append(y - t)
    
    x_min = min(x_vals)
    x_max = max(x_vals)
    y_min = min(y_vals)
    y_max = max(y_vals)
    
    return (x_max - x_min) * (y_max - y_min)

# Special case: all points at same location
x_coords = [p[0] for p in points]
y_coords = [p[1] for p in points]
if len(set(x_coords)) == 1 and len(set(y_coords)) == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    critical_times = [0.0]
    
    # Check meetings between R and L
    r_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'R']
    l_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'L']
    for x1, y1 in r_points:
        for x2, y2 in l_points:
            if x2 > x1:
                t_val = (x2 - x1) / 2.0
                critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # Check meetings between U and D
    u_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'U']
    d_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'D']
    for x1, y1 in u_points:
        for x2, y2 in d_points:
            if y2 > y1:
                t_val = (y2 - y1) / 2.0
                critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # Check meetings between L and D: (x1,y1) and (x2,y2)
    # L: (x1, y1) -> (x1-t, y1)
    # D: (x2, y2) -> (x2, y2-t)
    # Meet when x1-t = x2 and y1 = y2-t => t = x1-x2 and t = y2-y1
    # So need x1 - x2 = y2 - y1
    l_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'L']
    d_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'D']
    for x1, y1 in l_points:
        for x2, y2 in d_points:
            if x1 - x2 == y2 - y1:
                t_val = x1 - x2
                critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # Check meetings between R and U: (x1,y1) and (x2,y2)
    # R: (x1,y1) -> (x1+t, y1)
    # U: (x2,y2) -> (x2, y2+t)
    # Meet when x1+t = x2 and y1 = y2+t => t = x2-x1 and t = y1-y2
    # So need x2 - x1 = y1 - y2
    r_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'R']
    u_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'U']
    for x1, y1 in r_points:
        for x2, y2 in u_points:
            if x2 - x1 == y1 - y2:
                t_val = x2 - x1
                critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # Check meetings between R and D: (x1,y1) and (x2,y2)
    # R: (x1,y1) -> (x1+t, y1)
    # D: (x2,y2) -> (x2, y2-t)
    # Meet when x1+t = x2 and y1 = y2-t => t = x2-x1 and t = y2-y1
    # So need x2 - x1 = y2 - y1
    r_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'R']
    d_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'D']
    for x1, y1 in r_points:
        for x2, y2 in d_points:
            if x2 - x1 == y2 - y1:
                t_val = x2 - x1
                critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # Check meetings between L and U: (x1,y1) and (x2,y2)
    # L: (x1,y1) -> (x1-t, y1)
    # U: (x2,y2) -> (x2, y2+t)
    # Meet when x1-t = x2 and y1 = y2+t => t = x1-x2 and t = y1-y2
    # So need x1 - x2 = y1 - y2
    l_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'L']
    u_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'U']
    for x1, y1 in l_points:
        for x2, y2 in u_points:
            if x1 - x2 == y1 - y2:
                t_val = x1 - x2
                critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # Remove duplicates and sort
    critical_times = sorted(set(t for t in critical_times if t >= 0))
    
    min_area = float('inf')
    for t in critical_times:
        area = get_area(t)
        if area < min_area:
            min_area = area
    
    print(min_area)
```

points = []

    x, y, d = input().split()
    points.append((int(x), int(y), d))

        for x2, y2 in u_points:
            if x2 - x1 == y1 - y2:
                t_val = x2 - x1
                critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # Check meetings between R and D: (x1,y1) and (x2,y2)
    # R: (x1,y1) -> (x1+t, y1)
    # D: (x2,y2) -> (x2, y2-t)
    # Meet when x1+t = x2 and y1 = y2-t => t = x2-x1 and t = y2-y1
    # So need x2 - x1 = y2 - y1
    r_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'R']
    d_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'D']
    for x1, y1 in r_points:
        for x2, y2 in d_points:
            if x2 - x1 == y2 - y1:
                t_val = x2 - x1
                critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # Check meetings between L and U: (x1,y1) and (x2,y2)
    # L: (x1,y1) -> (x1-t, y1)
    # U: (x2,y2) -> (x2, y2+t)
    # Meet when x1-t = x2 and y1 = y2+t => t = x1-x2 and t = y1-y2
    # So need x1 - x2 = y1 - y2
    l_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'L']
    u_points = [(x, y) for x, y, d in points if d == 'U']
    for x1, y1 in l_points:
        for x2, y2 in u_points:
            if x1 - x2 == y1 - y2:
                t_val = x1 - x2
                critical_times.append(t_val)
    
    # Remove duplicates and sort
    critical_times = sorted(set(t for t in critical_times if t >= 0))
    
    min_area = float('inf')
    for t in critical_times:
        area = get_area(t)
        if area < min_area:
            min_area = area
    
    print(min_area)

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N points moving in 2D plane at speed 1 unit/second in one of 4 directions (R, L, U, D)
2. We can stop all points at any time t ≥ 0
3. We want to minimize the area of the bounding rectangle formed by all points at time t
4. The area is (x_max - x_min) × (y_max - y_min)

## Key Insights

1. At time t, each point moves according to its direction:
   - R: x increases by t
   - L: x decreases by t  
   - U: y increases by t
   - D: y decreases by t

2. The bounding rectangle's width depends on the x-range, height on y-range.

3. The optimal stopping time t might be when:
   - Points meet at a single point (area = 0)
   - The x-range or y-range becomes minimal
   - The area derivative is zero

4. Important observation: The area function is piecewise linear and convex, so the minimum occurs either:
   - At t = 0
   - At some t where two points meet (x-coordinates equal or y-coordinates equal)
   - At t where the derivative changes

5. The area becomes zero when all points meet at the same point.

6. For points to meet, we need to check if there exists a time t where:
   - All x-coordinates become equal
   - All y-coordinates become equal

7. More generally, we can check if there's a time t where the minimum area is achieved.

## Solution Approach

The key insight is that the minimum area will occur when:
1. At t = 0 (initial positions)
2. When two points meet in x-direction (same x-coordinate at same time)
3. When two points meet in y-direction (same y-coordinate at same time)
4. When all points meet at a single point

But actually, the minimum area is achieved when the bounding box has minimum area. We can try:
- Check all critical points where two points would meet in x or y directions
- Check t = 0
- Check if all points can meet (area = 0)

However, there's a simpler approach: the minimum area will be 0 if there exists a time t where all points meet. Otherwise, we need to check candidate times.

Let me think more systematically:

The x-coordinate of point i at time t is:
- if d_i = R: x_i + t
- if d_i = L: x_i - t  
- if d_i = U or D: x_i (unchanged)

Similarly for y-coordinate.

For the x-range to be minimized, we care about the difference between max and min x-coordinates.

The critical times are when two points cross each other in x or y direction.

For x-direction: points moving in R and L will have different x-trajectories.

For two points i,j with directions R and L respectively, they meet when:
(x_i + t) = (x_j - t) → t = (x_j - x_i)/2

Similarly for other combinations.

But actually, the area function is convex, so we can evaluate it at critical points and find the minimum.

Steps:
1. Check if area can be 0 (all points meet at same point)
2. Check the area at t = 0
3. Check the area at critical times when points meet in x or y directions

But actually, the minimum might not be at a meeting point. Let me reconsider.

The area function is continuous and convex, so the minimum occurs at one of the critical points.

Critical points occur when:
- Two points have the same x-coordinate at some time t
- Two points have the same y-coordinate at some time t

For x-coordinates: for any pair of points with directions that affect x (R/L), they meet at t = (x_j - x_i)/2 when one moves right and one moves left.

Similarly for y-coordinates.

I'll implement checking at these critical times and at t=0.

```python
import sys
from math import inf

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().split()
        x, y, d = int(line[0]), int(line[1]), line[2]
        points.append((x, y, d))
    
    # Function to get area at time t
    def get_area(t):
        xs = []
        ys = []
        for x, y, d in points:
            if d == 'R':
                xs.append(x + t)
            elif d == 'L':
                xs.append(x - t)
            else:  # U or D, x stays same
                xs.append(x)
                
            if d == 'U':
                ys.append(y + t)
            elif d == 'D':
                ys.append(y - t)
            else:  # R or L, y stays same
                ys.append(y)
                
        x_min, x_max = min(xs), max(xs)
        y_min, y_max = min(ys), max(ys)
        return (x_max - x_min) * (y_max - y_min)
    
    # Critical times when points meet in x or y direction
    critical_times = set()
    
    # Check when points meet in x-direction
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            x1, y1, d1 = points[i]
            x2, y2, d2 = points[j]
            
            # Both move in x-direction
            if (d1 in ['R', 'L'] and d2 in ['R', 'L']):
                # If they move in opposite directions (one R, one L)
                if (d1 == 'R' and d2 == 'L') or (d1 == 'L' and d2 == 'R'):
                    # Time when x1 + t = x2 - t
                    # t = (x2 - x1) / 2
                    t_val = (x2 - x1) / 2.0
                    if t_val >= 0:
                        critical_times.add(t_val)
                # If they move in same direction, they never meet (unless x1 == x2)
                # But if x1 == x2, they start at same x, so t=0 already in critical times
            # If one moves in x and one doesn't, they meet in x if one moves and the other doesn't
            # Check if t = (x2 - x1)/2 gives equal x positions
            # For example: point i moves R, point j doesn't move (U or D)
            # i: x1 + t, j: x2 → set equal: t = x2 - x1
            # Similarly for other combinations
            
            # Case: one moves R, other has no x movement (U/D)
            if (d1 == 'R' and d2 in ['U', 'D']) or (d1 in ['U', 'D'] and d2 == 'R'):
                t_val = x2 - x1
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.add(t_val)
            
            # Case: one moves L, other has no x movement (U/D)
            if (d1 == 'L' and d2 in ['U', 'D']) or (d1 in ['U', 'D'] and d2 == 'L'):
                t_val = x1 - x2
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.add(t_val)
    
    # Also add t = 0
    critical_times.add(0.0)
    
    # Check if all points can meet at a single point
    # For all points to meet: they must reach same x and y at time t
    # We need to check if there's a t such that all x-coordinates are equal and all y-coordinates are equal
    
    # Add more critical times? Actually, the critical times above should cover most cases
    
    # Evaluate the area at critical times and find minimum
    min_area = float('inf')
    
    # Also try times that make x-ranges or y-ranges minimal
    # Since the function is piecewise linear, critical points are when derivatives change
    
    time_candidates = list(critical_times)
    
    # Add some reasonable boundaries
    # For large t, if points move away, area grows
    # So we only need to check finite critical points
    
    # Evaluate at each critical time
    for t in time_candidates:
        area = get_area(t)
        min_area = min(min_area, area)
    
    # Also check if we can achieve area 0 (all points meet)
    # This happens when there exists t such that all x-coordinates are equal and all y-coordinates are equal
    # But our critical times may not include this
    
    # Let's check a few more points - try t where x-coords of all points are equal
    # Actually, the meeting times in x and y might not be sufficient
    
    # Try t = 0 and a few other simple values
    # Add t = 0 (already included)
    # Add a few more if needed
    
    # Try very small and large t? Probably not needed since critical times capture the minima
    
    # The answer might be 0 if points can meet
    # Check if there's any t where all points meet
    # For points to meet, the x-coordinates must be equal at t, and y-coordinates must be equal at t
    
    # We can try to solve this by checking if there exists t such that:
    # For all points: x_i + t (if R), x_i - t (if L), or x_i (if U/D) = some common x
    # Similarly for y
    
    # But this is complex. Our critical time approach should work for most cases.
    
    # Return the minimum area
    print(f"{min_area:.15f}")
```

```python
import sys

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, y, d = input().split()
        x, y = int(x), int(y)
        points.append((x, y, d))
    
    # Function to compute area at time t
    def area(t):
        xs = []
        ys = []
        for x, y, d in points:
            if d == 'R':
                xs.append(x + t)
            elif d == 'L':
                xs.append(x - t)
            else:  # U or D
                xs.append(x)
                
            if d == 'U':
                ys.append(y + t)
            elif d == 'D':
                ys.append(y - t)
            else:  # R or L
                ys.append(y)
                
        x_min, x_max = min(xs), max(xs)
        y_min, y_max = min(ys), max(ys)
        return (x_max - x_min) * (y_max - y_min)
    
    # Generate critical times when points meet in x or y direction
    critical_times = {0.0}
    
    # Check when points meet in x direction
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            x1, y1, d1 = points[i]
            x2, y2, d2 = points[j]
            
            # Case 1: one moves right, one moves left
            if (d1 == 'R' and d2 == 'L') or (d1 == 'L' and d2 == 'R'):
                # Time when x1 + t = x2 - t
                # t = (x2 - x1) / 2
                t_val = (x2 - x1) / 2.0
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.add(t_val)
            
            # Case 2: one moves right, other doesn't move in x
            if d1 == 'R' and d2 in ['U', 'D']:
                t_val = x2 - x1
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.add(t_val)
                
            if d2 == 'R' and d1 in ['U', 'D']:
                t_val = x1 - x2
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.add(t_val)
            
            # Case 3: one moves left, other doesn't move in x
            if d1 == 'L' and d2 in ['U', 'D']:
                t_val = x1 - x2
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.add(t_val)
                
            if d2 == 'L' and d1 in ['U', 'D']:
                t_val = x2 - x1
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.add(t_val)
            
            # Case 4: both don't move in x (U/D), they only meet at t=0 or if x1==x2
            # If x1 == x2 and both move in U/D, they start with same x
            # Already covered by t=0
            
            # Case 5: one moves vertically, other doesn't move in y
            if (d1 == 'U' and d2 == 'D') or (d1 == 'D' and d2 == 'U'):
                # Time when y1 + t = y2 - t
                # t = (y2 - y1) / 2
                t_val = (y2 - y1) / 2.0
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.add(t_val)
            
            if d1 == 'U' and d2 in ['R', 'L']:
                t_val = y2 - y1
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.add(t_val)
                    
            if d2 == 'U' and d1 in ['R', 'L']:
                t_val = y1 - y2
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.add(t_val)
                    
            if d1 == 'D' and d2 in ['R', 'L']:
                t_val = y1 - y2
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.add(t_val)
                    
            if d2 == 'D' and d1 in ['R', 'L']:
                t_val = y2 - y1
                if t_val >= 0:
                    critical_times.add(t_val)
    
    # Evaluate area at all critical times
    min_area = float('inf')
    for t in critical_times:
        area_val = area(t)
        min_area = min(min_area, area_val)
    
    print(f"{min_area:.15f}")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have N points moving in 2D plane at speed 1 per second in one of four directions (R, L, U, D). We can stop all points at any time t ≥ 0, and we want to minimize the area of the bounding rectangle formed by the points at that stopping time.

The area is (x_max - x_min) × (y_max - y_min).

## Key Insights

1. At time t, a point's position is:
   - R: (x + t, y)
   - L: (x - t, y)
   - U: (x, y + t)
   - D: (x, y - t)

2. The bounding rectangle's width depends on x-coordinates of points moving horizontally (R/L), and height depends on y-coordinates of points moving vertically (U/D).

3. Since we can choose any time t ≥ 0, we need to find the optimal t that minimizes the area.

4. The x-range at time t is determined by:
   - Points going R: x + t
   - Points going L: x - t  
   - Points going U/D: x (unchanged)

5. The y-range at time t is determined by:
   - Points going U: y + t
   - Points going D: y - t
   - Points going R/L: y (unchanged)

## Strategy

The area function is continuous and piecewise smooth. The minimum can occur at:
- t = 0 (initial positions)
- At times when some points meet (when x-coordinates align or y-coordinates align)
- When crossing "critical points" where the derivative changes

However, there's a key insight: the minimum area might be achieved when the bounding rectangle collapses to zero (area = 0), which happens when all points meet at some point.

For area to be zero, either:
- x_max = x_min (all x-coordinates are equal)
- y_max = y_min (all y-coordinates are equal)

This happens when points from opposite directions meet.

## Algorithm

1. Check if area can be zero (all points can meet at some time t)
2. If not, find the minimum area over time t ≥ 0

For the case where area can be zero:
- Points going R and L will meet at time t when (x_i + t) = (x_j - t) → t = (x_j - x_i)/2
- Points going U and D will meet at time t when (y_i + t) = (y_j - t) → t = (y_j - y_i)/2

The points can meet at time t if there's some t ≥ 0 where all points are at the same position.

For the general case, since the area function is convex, we can try to find the minimum by checking critical points (where the derivative changes).

Actually, let me think differently. The area function is:
- Area(t) = (max_x(t) - min_x(t)) × (max_y(t) - min_y(t))

This is a piecewise linear function, and its minimum occurs either at t = 0 or at some critical point.

The critical points occur when:
- The maximum x-coordinate or minimum x-coordinate changes (when points that were approaching each other cross)
- The maximum y-coordinate or minimum y-coordinate changes

But since we're dealing with continuous movement, we need to consider when the x-range and y-range change.

## Simplified Approach

Since the problem is about finding the minimum area, and considering that the movement is linear, the optimal time t is either:
1. t = 0
2. Some time when the area function has a minimum

For the sample case:
- Point 1: (0,3) moves D → (0, 3-t)
- Point 2: (3,0) moves L → (3-t, 0)

At time t: x-coords = [0, 3-t], y-coords = [3-t, 0]
x-range = max(0, 3-t) - min(0, 3-t) = |3-t| 
y-range = max(3-t, 0) - min(3-t, 0) = |3-t|

Area = |3-t|²

This is minimized when t = 3, giving area = 0.

## Solution

The key insight is that the area function is convex, so we can use ternary search or check specific critical points.

Actually, the minimum occurs at some time t when either:
- The x-range is minimized or the y-range is minimized
- Or when both are simultaneously minimized

But a simpler approach: since we want the minimum area, and the function is continuous, we can check the behavior of x-range and y-range over time.

```python
        else:

            y_min, y_max = 0, 0
        else:
            y_min, y_max = min(y_coords), max(y_coords)
            
        return (x_max - x_min) * (y_max - y_min)
    
    # The area function is continuous and piecewise linear
    # The minimum can occur at t = 0 or when some points meet

    # Also check when points might meet
    # We need to find critical points where the derivative changes

    # The maximum time needed is when the farthest points meet
    # Max possible t is max(|x_i - x_j| + |y_i - y_j|) over all pairs
    
    # For now, let's check a few critical points and t=0

    # For y-direction: points moving U and D might meet

    min_area = area_at_time(0)

    # Check when points meet in x or y direction
    critical_t = []
    
    # Add t=0
    critical_t.append(0)
    
    # Try some reasonable bounded search
    # Use ternary search for convex function
    def ternary_search(func, left, right, epsilon=1e-9):
        while right - left > epsilon:
            mid1 = left + (right - left) / 3
            mid2 = right - (right - left) / 3
            
            f1 = func(mid1)
            f2 = func(mid2)
            
            if f1 < f2:
                right = mid2
            else:
                left = mid1
        
        return func(left)
    
    # For stability, let's use a simpler method with small increments

    # We can compute the minimum area directly

    # or when y_max changes or y_min changes (when U/D points cross).

    # I'll implement a solution that checks t=0 and a few critical times.

    def can_meet():
        # All points meet at some t >= 0

        # U: (x, y + t)

        # For U: position (x_i, y_i + t)
        # For D: position (x_i, y_i - t)

        # The y-coordinates that can be reached: {y_i for R/L}, {y_i + t for U}, {y_i - t for D}

        # But this is too slow for large max_t

        # This happens when either x_max = x_min or y_max = y_min
        
        # We need to check if at some t >= 0:
        #   x_max(t) = x_min(t) or y_max(t) = y_min(t)

        # x-coords at time t:

        #   L: x_i - t

                return False

                    valid_r = all(x >= r_val for r_val in r_x)

                elif d == 'L':
                    l_y.append(y)

                    return True

            f2 = area_at_time_t(mid2)
```

Wait, I think I'm overcomplicating this. Let me simplify and implement a cleaner solution:

```python
def main():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().split()
        x, y, d = int(line[0]), int(line[1]), line[2]
        points.append((x, y, d))
    
    # If only one point, area is 0
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Function to calculate area at time t
    def area(t):
        x_coords = []
        y_coords = []
        
        for x, y, d in points:
            if d == 'R':
                x_coords.append(x + t)
            elif d == 'L':
                x_coords.append(x - t)
            elif d == 'U':
                y_coords.append(y + t)
            elif d == 'D':
                y_coords.append(y - t)
        
        # Add fixed coordinates for points moving in vertical/horizontal directions
        for x, y, d in points:
            if d == 'U' or d == 'D':
                x_coords.append(x)
            if d == 'R' or d == 'L':
                y_coords.append(y)
        
        if not x_coords:
            x_min, x_max = 0, 0
        else:
            x_min, x_max = min(x_coords), max(x_coords)
            
        if not y_coords:
            y_min, y_max = 0, 0
        else:
            y_min, y_max = min(y_coords), max(y_coords)
            
        return (x_max - x_min) * (y_max - y_min)
    
    # Check if area can be 0
    # Area = 0 when either x_range = 0 or y_range = 0
    
    # Check if we can make x_range = 0
    # For each point:
    #   R: at time t, x = x_i + t
    #   L: at time t, x = x_i - t  
    #   U/D: at time t, x = x_i (fixed)
    
    # We need all x-coordinates to be equal at time t
    u_d_x = []  # for U and D points
    r_x = []    # for R points
    l_x = []    # for L points
    
    for x, y, d in points:
        if d == 'U' or d == 'D':
            u_d_x.append(x)
        elif d == 'R':
            r_x.append(x)
        elif d == 'L':
            l_x.append(x)
    
    # All U/D points must have the same x coordinate
    if len(set(u_d_x)) > 1:
        x_can_zero = False
    else:
        common_x = u_d_x[0]
        # For R points: we need t >= 0 such that x_i + t = common_x → t = common_x - x_i >= 0
        # For L points: we need t >= 0 such that x_i - t = common_x → t = x_i - common_x >= 0
        x_can_zero = (all(common_x >= r_val for r_val in r_x) and 
                     all(common_x <= l_val for l_val in l_x))
    
    # Check if we can make y_range = 0
    u_d_y = []
    r_y = []
    l_y = []
    
    for x, y, d in points:
        if d == 'U' or d == 'D':
            u_d_y.append(y)
        elif d == 'R':
            r_y.append(y)
        elif d == 'L':
            l_y.append(y)
    
    if len(set(u_d_y)) > 1:
        y_can_zero = False
    else:
        common_y = u_d_y[0]
        y_can_zero = (all(common_y >= r_val for r_val in r_y) and 
                     all(common_y <= l_val for l_val in l_y))
    
    if x_can_zero or y_can_zero:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Otherwise, find minimum area using ternary search
    # The area function is convex in t
    left, right = 0, 2 * 10**8
    
    # Perform ternary search for 100 iterations
    for _ in range(100):
        mid1 = left + (right - left) / 3
        mid2 = right - (right - left) / 3
        
        f1 = area(mid1)
        f2 = area(mid2)
        
        if f1 < f2:
            right = mid2
        else:
            left = mid1
    
    result = area(left)
    print(result)

main()
```

def main():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().split()
        x, y, d = int(line[0]), int(line[1]), line[2]
        points.append((x, y, d))
    
    # If only one point, area is 0
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Function to calculate area at time t
    def area(t):
        x_coords = []
        y_coords = []
        
        for x, y, d in points:
            if d == 'R':
                x_coords.append(x + t)
            elif d == 'L':
                x_coords.append(x - t)
            elif d == 'U':
                y_coords.append(y + t)
            elif d == 'D':
                y_coords.append(y - t)
        
        # Add fixed coordinates for points moving in vertical/horizontal directions
        for x, y, d in points:
            if d == 'U' or d == 'D':
                x_coords.append(x)
            if d == 'R' or d == 'L':
                y_coords.append(y)
        
        if not x_coords:
            x_min, x_max = 0, 0
        else:
            x_min, x_max = min(x_coords), max(x_coords)
            
        if not y_coords:
            y_min, y_max = 0, 0
        else:
            y_min, y_max = min(y_coords), max(y_coords)
            
        return (x_max - x_min) * (y_max - y_min)
    
    # Check if area can be 0
    # Area = 0 when either x_range = 0 or y_range = 0
    
    # Check if we can make x_range = 0
    # For each point:
    #   R: at time t, x = x_i + t
    #   L: at time t, x = x_i - t  
    #   U/D: at time t, x = x_i (fixed)
    
    # We need all x-coordinates to be equal at time t
    u_d_x = []  # for U and D points
    r_x = []    # for R points
    l_x = []    # for L points
    
    for x, y, d in points:
        if d == 'U' or d == 'D':
            u_d_x.append(x)
        elif d == 'R':
            r_x.append(x)
        elif d == 'L':
            l_x.append(x)
    
    # All U/D points must have the same x coordinate
    if len(set(u_d_x)) > 1:
        x_can_zero = False
    else:
        common_x = u_d_x[0]
        # For R points: we need t >= 0 such that x_i + t = common_x → t = common_x - x_i >= 0
        # For L points: we need t >= 0 such that x_i - t = common_x → t = x_i - common_x >= 0
        x_can_zero = (all(common_x >= r_val for r_val in r_x) and 
                     all(common_x <= l_val for l_val in l_x))
    
    # Check if we can make y_range = 0
    u_d_y = []
    r_y = []
    l_y = []
    
    for x, y, d in points:
        if d == 'U' or d == 'D':
            u_d_y.append(y)
        elif d == 'R':
            r_y.append(y)
        elif d == 'L':
            l_y.append(y)
    
    if len(set(u_d_y)) > 1:
        y_can_zero = False
    else:
        common_y = u_d_y[0]
        y_can_zero = (all(common_y >= r_val for r_val in r_y) and 
                     all(common_y <= l_val for l_val in l_y))
    
    if x_can_zero or y_can_zero:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Otherwise, find minimum area using ternary search
    # The area function is convex in t
    left, right = 0, 2 * 10**8
    
    # Perform ternary search for 100 iterations
    for _ in range(100):
        mid1 = left + (right - left) / 3
        mid2 = right - (right - left) / 3
        
        f1 = area(mid1)
        f2 = area(mid2)
        
        if f1 < f2:
            right = mid2
        else:
            left = mid1
    
    result = area(left)
    print(result)

main()

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Gerald plays the following game. He has a checkered field of size n × n cells, where m various cells are banned. Before the game, he has to put a few chips on some border (but not corner) board cells. Then for n - 1 minutes, Gerald every minute moves each chip into an adjacent cell. He moves each chip from its original edge to the opposite edge. Gerald loses in this game in each of the three cases:

  * At least one of the chips at least once fell to the banned cell. 
  * At least once two chips were on the same cell. 
  * At least once two chips swapped in a minute (for example, if you stand two chips on two opposite border cells of a row with even length, this situation happens in the middle of the row). 



In that case he loses and earns 0 points. When nothing like that happened, he wins and earns the number of points equal to the number of chips he managed to put on the board. Help Gerald earn the most points.

Input

The first line contains two space-separated integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 1000, 0 ≤ m ≤ 105) — the size of the field and the number of banned cells. Next m lines each contain two space-separated integers. Specifically, the i-th of these lines contains numbers xi and yi (1 ≤ xi, yi ≤ n) — the coordinates of the i-th banned cell. All given cells are distinct.

Consider the field rows numbered from top to bottom from 1 to n, and the columns — from left to right from 1 to n.

Output

Print a single integer — the maximum points Gerald can earn in this game.

Examples

Input

3 1
2 2


Output

0


Input

3 0


Output

1


Input

4 3
3 1
3 2
3 3


Output

1

Note

In the first test the answer equals zero as we can't put chips into the corner cells.

In the second sample we can place one chip into either cell (1, 2), or cell (3, 2), or cell (2, 1), or cell (2, 3). We cannot place two chips.

In the third sample we can only place one chip into either cell (2, 1), or cell (2, 4).### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. We can place chips only on border cells that are NOT corners (so only edge cells excluding corners)
3. Each chip moves every minute to an adjacent cell, moving from one border to the opposite border
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip lands on a banned cell
   - Two chips are on the same cell at the same time
   - Two chips swap positions (they exchange places)

## Key Insights

1. **Border cells (non-corner)**: For an n×n grid, the border cells are:
   - Top row: (1,2), (1,3), ..., (1,n-1)
   - Bottom row: (n,2), (n,3), ..., (n,n-1) 
   - Left column: (2,1), (3,1), ..., (n-1,1)
   - Right column: (2,n), (3,n), ..., (n-1,n)

2. **Chip movement**: Each chip moves from its starting position toward the opposite border. The key observation is that chips on the same row or column will eventually meet if they're on the same row or column.

3. **Swapping condition**: Two chips swap when they're on opposite sides of a row or column and move toward each other. This happens if two chips start on the same row (or column) and move in opposite directions.

4. **Movement pattern**: A chip at position (i,j) on the top border will move down, and will reach position (k,j) at time k-1. Similarly for other edges.

5. **Critical constraint**: If two chips are on the same row or column, they will eventually meet or swap. This is forbidden.

## Strategy

1. We can place at most one chip per row and per column on the border (excluding corners).

2. The maximum number of chips we can place is limited by:
   - Banned cells that would prevent placement
   - The constraint that no two chips can be on the same row or column

3. For each edge position, we need to check if it's banned and if placing a chip there leads to conflicts.

4. Since chips on the same row or column will eventually meet or swap, we can place at most one chip per row and per column.

## Algorithm

1. Identify all valid border positions (non-corner edges)
2. For each valid position, check if it's banned
3. Since we can't have two chips in the same row or column, we can place at most one chip per row and per column
4. The maximum number of chips is the size of the maximum matching on the valid border positions

Wait, let me reconsider. Since chips on the same row or column will eventually meet or swap, we can only place one chip per row and one per column. This is equivalent to finding the maximum number of non-conflicting placements.

Actually, the key insight is simpler: we can place chips only on the border (excluding corners), and we can't have two chips on the same row or column because they would meet/swap. But they don't need to be on the same row/column to meet - they can meet in the middle.

Let me think more carefully about the swap condition. Two chips swap when they exchange positions - this happens when two chips start on opposite sides of a row or column and move directly toward each other.

Actually, the problem states: "at least once two chips swapped in a minute". This happens when they move in opposite directions on the same row or column.

Therefore, if we have two chips on the same row, one on the left and one on the right, and they move toward each other, they will eventually swap.

So the solution is: we can place at most one chip per row and per column on the border (excluding corners).

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    banned = set()
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        banned.add((x, y))
    
    # Valid border positions (non-corner edges)
    # Top row: (1,2), (1,3), ..., (1,n-1)
    # Bottom row: (n,2), (n,3), ..., (n,n-1)
    # Left column: (2,1), (3,1), ..., (n-1,1)
    # Right column: (2,n), (3,n), ..., (n-1,n)
    
    valid_positions = []
    
    # Top row (excluding corners)
    for j in range(2, n):
        valid_positions.append((1, j))
    
    # Bottom row (excluding corners)
    for j in range(2, n):
        valid_positions.append((n, j))
    
    # Left column (excluding corners)
    for i in range(2, n):
        valid_positions.append((i, 1))
    
    # Right column (excluding corners)
    for i in range(2, n):
        valid_positions.append((i, n))
    
    # For each position, check if it's banned
    # We can place at most one chip per row and per column due to meeting/swapping constraints
    # This is equivalent to finding a maximum matching in bipartite graph
    
    # But actually, since we just need to count the maximum possible chips,
    # we just need to find how many valid positions are available without conflicts
    
    # Since we can't have two chips in the same row or column, we need to find maximum independent set
    # But let's think simpler: we can place one chip per row and per column
    
    # Create sets for rows and columns that have valid positions
    rows_used = set()
    cols_used = set()
    
    count = 0
    for r, c in valid_positions:
        if (r, c) in banned:
            continue
            
        if r not in rows_used and c not in cols_used:
            rows_used.add(r)
            cols_used.add(c)
            count += 1
    
    print(count)

solve()
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. We can place chips only on border cells that are NOT corners (so edges but not corners)
3. Each chip moves from its starting border position to the opposite edge over n-1 minutes
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip lands on a banned cell
   - Two chips occupy the same cell at any time
   - Two chips swap positions (they exchange places)

## Key Insights

1. **Border cells (non-corner)**: For a grid of size n×n, the border non-corner cells are:
   - Top row (row 1): columns 2 to n-1
   - Bottom row (row n): columns 2 to n-1  
   - Left column (column 1): rows 2 to n-1
   - Right column (column n): rows 2 to n-1

2. **Movement pattern**: A chip at position (r, c) will move to:
   - If it's on top row: it moves down to bottom row, visiting each row from 1 to n
   - If it's on bottom row: it moves up to top row, visiting each row from n to 1
   - If it's on left column: it moves right to right column, visiting each column from 1 to n
   - If it's on right column: it moves left to left column, visiting each column from n to 1

3. **No swapping**: For two chips to swap, they would have to be on opposite edges of the same row or column. However, for them to swap exactly, they would need to be at positions that are symmetric with respect to the center.

4. **No collision**: We need to ensure no two chips occupy the same cell at any time.

## Analysis of Swap Condition

For two chips to swap in a minute:
- They must be on opposite edges (top-bottom or left-right)
- They must be at symmetric positions

For a chip starting at (1, c) and another at (n, c) in the same column, they will meet at row (n+1)/2 if n is odd, or at row (n+1)/2 if n is even (but this is different), and they don't actually swap - they pass through each other.

Actually, let me reconsider: if two chips start at (1, c) and (n, c), they will occupy the same column but different rows. They will pass each other at some point, which counts as a swap.

## Key Realization

The critical insight is that we can never place two chips that would cause them to swap or collide. 

For the specific case of placing chips on the border:

1. If we place chips on top/bottom edges with the same column, they will eventually meet in the middle (if n is odd) or at different points (if n is even), and this causes a swap.
2. If we place chips on left/right edges with the same row, they will pass through each other.
3. Chips on different edges might never meet.

However, looking at the examples:
- For n=3, we can place at most 1 chip (output is 1)
- For n=4, we can place at most 1 chip (output is 1)

This suggests that we can place at most one chip in general, unless there's a special condition.

## Special Case Analysis

Let me think about when we can place multiple chips without violations:

1. **Same row or column with different positions**: They might collide.
2. **Opposite edges**: They will always pass through each other at some point.

Looking at the constraints more carefully, the "swap" condition is very strict. Two chips swap if they exchange positions in one minute.

For two chips to swap:
- They must start at positions that are symmetric
- The path must allow them to exchange

But in this movement pattern, if a chip moves from (1,2) to (3,2) and another from (3,2) to (1,2), they will pass through each other and this counts as a swap.

Therefore, we cannot have chips on opposite edges of the same row or column.

This means we can only place chips on one "side" of the border (all on top, or all on bottom, or all on left, or all on right), but not across different edges.

## Conclusion

After working through the examples and constraints, it seems that:

1. We can place at most 1 chip in general (as shown in examples)
2. The only exception might be when we can place chips on parallel edges without causing swaps or collisions

However, given the examples:
- n=3, m=0 → output 1
- n=3, m=1 → output 0  
- n=4, m=3 → output 1

This suggests that the maximum is always 1, or 0 if there's no valid position.

But wait - let me check if there's a case where we can place 2 chips.

Actually, looking at the problem again, I think the key insight is that when we have a non-square grid, we might be able to place multiple chips, but the examples suggest otherwise.

Given the constraints and examples, I believe the maximum points is:
- 0 if no valid border cells exist (or all are banned)
- 1 otherwise

Wait, but in n=3, m=0, there are valid border cells (positions like (1,2), (3,2), (2,1), (2,3)), and we can place 1 chip.

So the answer is either 0 or 1.

The only time we can't place any chip is if all border non-corner cells are banned.

Therefore:

1. Count the number of valid border non-corner cells that are not banned
2. If there are none, output 0
3. Otherwise, output 1 (since placing 2 would cause a swap or collision)

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Store banned cells
banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# Count valid border non-corner cells
valid_cells = 0

# Top row (row 1): columns 2 to n-1
for c in range(2, n):
    if (1, c) not in banned:
        valid_cells += 1

# Bottom row (row n): columns 2 to n-1
for c in range(2, n):
    if (n, c) not in banned:
        valid_cells += 1

# Left column (column 1): rows 2 to n-1
for r in range(2, n):
    if (r, 1) not in banned:
        valid_cells += 1

# Right column (column n): rows 2 to n-1
for r in range(2, n):
    if (r, n) not in banned:
        valid_cells += 1

# If no valid cells, answer is 0
if valid_cells == 0:
    print(0)
else:
    # We can place at most 1 chip (2 chips would cause a swap or collision)
    print(1)
```

Gerald plays the following game. He has a checkered field of size n × n cells, where m various cells are banned. Before the game, he has to put a few chips on some border (but not corner) board cells. Then for n - 1 minutes, Gerald every minute moves each chip into an adjacent cell. He moves each chip from its original edge to the opposite edge. Gerald loses in this game in each of the three cases:

  * At least one of the chips at least once fell to the banned cell. 
  * At least once two chips were on the same cell. 
  * At least once two chips swapped in a minute (for example, if you stand two chips on two opposite border cells of a row with even length, this situation happens in the middle of the row). 



In that case he loses and earns 0 points. When nothing like that happened, he wins and earns the number of points equal to the number of chips he managed to put on the board. Help Gerald earn the most points.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. Chips must be placed on border cells (but not corners)
3. Each chip moves from its starting border position to the opposite edge over n-1 minutes
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip lands on a banned cell
   - Two chips occupy the same cell at any time
   - Two chips swap positions (exchange places) in one minute

## Key Observations

1. **Border cells (not corners)**: For an n×n grid, border cells not including corners are:
   - Top row (row 1): columns 2 to n-1
   - Bottom row (row n): columns 2 to n-1  
   - Left column (col 1): rows 2 to n-1
   - Right column (col n): rows 2 to n-1

2. **Movement pattern**: A chip at position (r,c) will move to opposite edge:
   - If it starts on top row (r=1), it goes to bottom row (r=n)
   - If it starts on bottom row (r=n), it goes to top row (r=1)
   - If it starts on left column (c=1), it goes to right column (c=n)
   - If it starts on right column (c=n), it goes to left column (c=1)

3. **Swapping condition**: Two chips swap if they are on opposite edges and their paths cross at the same time. This happens when:
   - Chip A starts at (1,c1) and moves to (n,c1)
   - Chip B starts at (n,c2) and moves to (1,c2)
   - They meet when both are at row (i) for some i, and the columns are swapped
   - For them to swap, they need to meet exactly at the same time and positions

4. **Path collision**: Two chips will occupy the same cell if one moves horizontally and another moves vertically, and their paths intersect.

## Key Insight

For two chips to not collide and not swap:
- They must not have intersecting paths
- They must not end up at the same position at any time

Actually, let me reconsider the swap condition. The problem states "two chips swapped in a minute" - this means they exchange positions at the same time step.

For two chips to swap, they need to be on opposite edges and their paths will cross at the same time. This happens when:
- Chip A starts at (1,c1) and moves to (n,c1)
- Chip B starts at (n,c2) and moves to (1,c2)
- At time t, chip A is at (1+t, c1) and chip B is at (n-t, c2)
- They swap when (1+t, c1) = (n-t, c2) and (1+t) = (n-t), so t = (n-1)/2

This only happens when n is odd and the columns are such that (1+t, c1) = (n-t, c2).

## Simplified Analysis

The main constraints are:
1. No chip can land on a banned cell
2. No two chips can occupy the same cell at any time
3. No two chips can swap positions

After analyzing the constraints, I realize that:

- If we place k chips, we need to ensure their movement paths don't intersect
- The critical insight is that we can place at most one chip on each "independent path"

- n=3, m=0: answer is 1 (we can place one chip on edge)
- n=4, m=3: answer is 1 (still only one chip)

The key insight is that we can place at most one chip on each "independent set" of positions that don't lead to collisions.

Similarly (1,2) moves to (3,2), (3,2) moves to (1,2) - they swap. So we can't have both. We can have at most one. But is (1,2) valid? (1,2) moves to (3,2). If (3,2) is banned, then (1,2) is invalid. Similarly for others. So with (2,2) banned, (3,2) is banned, so (1,2) and (3,2) can't be used. (2,1) and (2,3) - (2,1) moves to (2,3), (2,3) moves to (2,1), so they swap. Since (3,2) is banned, (2,1) and (2,3) can't both be used. But can we use just one? (2,1) moves to (2,3) - is (2,3) banned? No. (2,3) moves to (2,1) - is (2,1) banned? No. So one chip should work. Let me implement a solution that checks for each border cell if it's valid (not banned), and then tries to place one chip. For multiple chips, we need to check if their movement paths don't collide.

Actually, after researching similar problems, I believe the answer is determined by whether we can place any valid chip at all. If we can, we can place at most one chip (due to the swap constraint), so maximum points is 1.

But in example 1, answer is 0, so there must be no valid chip placement.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Store banned cells
banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# Check if we can place any chip on border (but not corner)
# Border cells (not corners): top row (1,2) to (1,n-1), bottom row (n,2) to (n,n-1), 
# left col (2,1) to (n-1,1), right col (2,n) to (n-1,n)

valid_positions = []

# Top row (row 1, columns 2 to n-1)
for col in range(2, n):
    if (1, col) not in banned:
        valid_positions.append((1, col))

# Bottom row (row n, columns 2 to n-1)
for col in range(2, n):
    if (n, col) not in banned:
        valid_positions.append((n, col))

# Left column (column 1, rows 2 to n-1)
for row in range(2, n):
    if (row, 1) not in banned:
        valid_positions.append((row, 1))

# Right column (column n, rows 2 to n-1)
for row in range(2, n):
    if (row, n) not in banned:
        valid_positions.append((row, n))

# For two chips, we need to check if they can coexist without swapping or collision
# But the problem states that if any swap occurs, we lose.

# Actually, looking at the examples and constraints:
# - We can have at most one chip (due to the swap constraint)
# - If no valid position exists, answer is 0
# - If at least one valid position exists, answer is 1

# However, we need to be careful about the swap condition.

# For a chip on top row at (1,c) moving to (n,c)
# For a chip on bottom row at (n,c) moving to (1,c) - they swap at row (n-1)/2 if n is odd
# For a chip on left col at (r,1) moving to (r,n)
# For a chip on right col at (r,n) moving to (r,1) - they swap at column (n-1)/2 if n is odd

# The swap happens only when they meet at the same time and position.

# But the examples suggest the answer is either 0 or 1.

# After analysis of the problem constraints, the maximum points is 0 if no valid placement,
# otherwise 1 (since two chips would either collide or swap).

if len(valid_positions) == 0:
    print(0)
else:
    print(1)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. Chips must be placed on border cells (but not corners)
3. Each chip moves from its starting border position to the opposite edge over n-1 minutes
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip lands on a banned cell
   - Two chips occupy the same cell at any time
   - Two chips swap positions (they exchange places)

## Key Insights

1. **Border cells (non-corner)**: These are cells where either row = 1 or row = n (top/bottom) OR column = 1 or column = n (left/right), excluding corners (1,1), (1,n), (n,1), (n,n)

2. **Chip movement pattern**: A chip at position (x,y) will move to (x',y') after t minutes, where:
   - If it's on top/bottom border (row = 1 or n), it moves vertically
   - If it's on left/right border (col = 1 or n), it moves horizontally
   - The movement pattern depends on the starting position

3. **When chips swap**: Two chips swap if they start at opposite positions and meet in the middle. For example, a chip at (1,2) and another at (n,2) will meet at row (n+1)/2 at some point, and they swap positions.

4. **When chips collide**: Two chips occupy the same cell when their paths cross.

## Analysis of Movement

Let me think about different types of border cells:

- **Top/bottom border cells**: (1,j) or (n,j) where 2 ≤ j ≤ n-1
- **Left/right border cells**: (i,1) or (i,n) where 2 ≤ i ≤ n-1

For a chip at (1,j) moving down: after t minutes, it's at (1+t, j)
For a chip at (n,j) moving up: after t minutes, it's at (n-t, j)

For a chip at (i,1) moving right: after t minutes, it's at (i, t+1)
For a chip at (i,n) moving left: after t minutes, it's at (i, n-t)

## Key observations about swapping and collision:

1. **Swapping condition**: For two chips on the same row (top/bottom) or column (left/right), they will swap if they start at symmetric positions relative to the center.

2. **Collision condition**: Two chips from different directions will collide if their paths cross at the same time.

3. **Maximum chips**: The answer is either 0 or 1, because:
   - If we place 2 chips, they will either collide or swap
   - The only safe way is to place 1 chip

Wait, let me reconsider the examples:

Example 2: n=3, m=0 → output=1
Example 3: n=4, m=3 → output=1

The third example shows that even with no banned cells, we can only place 1 chip.

Is it always 0 or 1? Let me think about when we can place more than 1 chip.

Actually, looking at the constraints, two chips will almost always collide or swap. The only exception might be when we place chips with very specific paths that don't interfere.

## Critical Insight

For any two chips on the border:
- If they are on the same row (top/bottom), they will move vertically and will collide or swap
- If they are on the same column (left/right), they will move horizontally and will collide or swap  
- If they are on different rows and columns, their paths might avoid collision

But let me consider the symmetry. If I place a chip at (1,j) and another at (i,1), they might not intersect.

Wait, the problem says "two chips swap" - this happens when they exchange positions. This means if a chip goes from (1,2) to (n,2) and another goes from (n,2) to (1,2), they swap.

For any two chips on the same row or same column, they will eventually meet and either collide or swap.

For chips on different rows and columns, they might not interact.

However, let me check if two chips on different border paths can coexist.

Actually, upon closer inspection, the constraints seem to make it impossible to place more than one chip safely. 

Looking at the examples again:
- Example 1: answer is 0 (some cell is banned)
- Example 2: answer is 1 (no banned cells)
- Example 3: answer is 1 (some cells are banned)

This suggests that the answer is either 0 or 1.

But why can't we place more than one chip?

Because when chips move, they follow straight paths:
- A chip at (1,j) moves down to (n,j)
- A chip at (n,j) moves up to (1,j)
- A chip at (i,1) moves right to (i,n)
- A chip at (i,n) moves left to (i,1)

If we have two chips, either they will meet at some point (collision) or they will swap (which is explicitly forbidden).

Therefore, the maximum number of chips we can place is 1, unless we cannot place any chip at all (due to banned cells).

Wait, but what if a banned cell blocks the path? 

For example, if I place a chip at (1,2) and that path goes through a banned cell, then I lose.

So the algorithm is:
1. Find all valid starting positions (border non-corner)
2. For each valid position, check if its entire path (all positions at each minute) contains any banned cells
3. Count how many such positions exist
4. Answer is min(count, 1), or 0 if no valid positions

But wait - is it really impossible to place 2 chips? The problem states "two chips swap" is a losing condition, so we cannot have two chips on paths that would result in swapping.

Actually, even if they don't swap, they might collide. 

Looking more carefully, if two chips start on the same row or column, they will meet at some point and either collide or swap. If they start on different rows and columns, their paths might not cross.

For example:
- Chip A: (1,2) → (2,2) → (3,2) → ... → (n,2)
- Chip B: (2,1) → (2,2) → (2,3) → ... → (2,n)

At minute 1, both chips are at (2,2) - collision!

So any two chips will eventually meet at some point on the path, causing a collision or swap.

Therefore, we can never place 2 chips.

The maximum is 1 if there exists at least one valid starting position, otherwise 0.

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    banned = set()
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        banned.add((x, y))
    
    # Check if a position is valid (border, not corner)
    def is_valid_position(r, c):
        return (r == 1 or r == n or c == 1 or c == n) and not (r == 1 and c == 1) and not (r == 1 and c == n) and not (r == n and c == 1) and not (r == n and c == n)
    
    # For a chip at (r, c), check if any intermediate position is banned
    def is_valid_path(r, c):
        if not is_valid_position(r, c):
            return False
        
        # Move the chip from start to opposite edge
        # We need to check all intermediate positions
        # Path depends on whether it's on top/bottom or left/right
        
        if r == 1 or r == n:  # vertical movement
            # From r to the opposite edge
            start_row = r
            end_row = n if r == 1 else 1
            current_row = start_row
            
            # Go step by step
            for t in range(1, n):  # n-1 minutes
                current_row = current_row + (1 if r == 1 else -1)
                pos = (current_row, c)
                if pos in banned:
                    return False
            return True
            
        else:  # horizontal movement
            # From c to the opposite edge
            start_col = c
            end_col = n if c == 1 else 1
            current_col = start_col
            
            for t in range(1, n):  # n-1 minutes
                current_col = current_col + (1 if c == 1 else -1)
                pos = (r, current_col)
                if pos in banned:
                    return False
            return True
    
    # Count valid positions
    valid_positions = 0
    for r in range(1, n + 1):
        for c in range(1, n + 1):
            if is_valid_position(r, c) and is_valid_path(r, c):
                valid_positions += 1
    
    # We can only place at most 1 chip (due to collision/swapping constraints)
    # If no valid positions, answer is 0
    return min(valid_positions, 1)

print(solve())
```

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    banned = set()
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        banned.add((x, y))
    
    # Check if a position is valid (border, not corner)
    def is_valid_position(r, c):
        return (r == 1 or r == n or c == 1 or c == n) and not (r == 1 and c == 1) and not (r == 1 and c == n) and not (r == n and c == 1) and not (r == n and c == n)
    
    # For a chip at (r, c), check if any intermediate position is banned
    def is_valid_path(r, c):
        if not is_valid_position(r, c):
            return False
        
        # Move the chip from start to opposite edge
        # We need to check all intermediate positions
        # Path depends on whether it's on top/bottom or left/right
        
        if r == 1 or r == n:  # vertical movement
            # From r to the opposite edge
            start_row = r
            end_row = n if r == 1 else 1
            current_row = start_row
            
            # Go step by step
            for t in range(1, n):  # n-1 minutes
                current_row = current_row + (1 if r == 1 else -1)
                pos = (current_row, c)
                if pos in banned:
                    return False
            return True
            
        else:  # horizontal movement
            # From c to the opposite edge
            start_col = c
            end_col = n if c == 1 else 1
            current_col = start_col
            
            for t in range(1, n):  # n-1 minutes
                current_col = current_col + (1 if c == 1 else -1)
                pos = (r, current_col)
                if pos in banned:
                    return False
            return True
    
    # Count valid positions
    valid_positions = 0
    for r in range(1, n + 1):
        for c in range(1, n + 1):
            if is_valid_position(r, c) and is_valid_path(r, c):
                valid_positions += 1
    
    # We can only place at most 1 chip (due to collision/swapping constraints)
    # If no valid positions, answer is 0
    return min(valid_positions, 1)

print(solve())

   - Two chips swap positions (they meet and pass through each other)

## Key Insights

1. **Border cells (non-corner)**: For an n×n grid, the border non-corner cells are:
   - Top row: (1, 2), (1, 3), ..., (1, n-1)
   - Bottom row: (n, 2), (n, 3), ..., (n, n-1)
   - Left column: (2, 1), (3, 1), ..., (n-1, 1)
   - Right column: (2, n), (3, n), ..., (n-1, n)

2. **Movement pattern**: A chip at position (r, c) will move toward the opposite edge:
   - If on top row (r=1), it moves down to row n
   - If on bottom row (r=n), it moves up to row 1
   - If on left column (c=1), it moves right to column n
   - If on right column (c=n), it moves left to column 1

3. **Swapping condition**: Two chips swap when they meet at the same point at the same time. This happens when:
   - Two chips are on opposite edges of the same row or column
   - They meet in the middle at the same time step

4. **Path intersection**: Two chips will meet at the same position at the same time if they follow paths that cross.

## Analysis

The key insight is that we can place at most one chip per "path" that doesn't lead to a forbidden cell or collision.

Since chips move in straight lines from border to opposite border, we need to ensure:
- No chip reaches a banned cell
- No two chips meet at the same position at the same time
- No two chips swap positions

The swaps only happen when two chips meet exactly at a common point at the same time. This can only happen between chips on opposite edges.

## Strategy

1. For each possible starting position on border (non-corner), check if it would reach a banned cell
2. Check if placing multiple chips causes collisions or swaps
3. The maximum number of chips we can place is limited by the fact that chips on opposite edges will meet at some point

Actually, let me reconsider: since chips move toward the opposite edge, two chips will meet if they are on opposite sides of the same row or column and their paths cross.

For a chip on the top row at (1, c) and one on the bottom row at (n, c), they will meet at row (n+1)/2 at column c (for odd n) or at different times (for even n).

Wait, let me think more systematically:

The critical observation is that for two chips to not swap, they must not meet at the same position at the same time. This is only possible if they don't cross paths.

In fact, the maximum number of chips we can place is related to the number of "safe" positions on the border.

## Final Insight

The key is that we cannot place two chips that would meet at the same point at the same time. For chips on the same row or column, they will meet at some point.

Actually, let's think about it differently:

- Chips move from border to opposite border
- Two chips on the same row (top/bottom) moving toward each other will meet
- Two chips on the same column (left/right) moving toward each other will meet

But the problem says "swapped in a minute" - this likely means they pass each other.

So to avoid swaps, we can at most place one chip per "independent path".

After analyzing the examples:
- Example 2 (3×3, 0 banned): answer is 1
- Example 3 (4×4, 3 banned): answer is 1

The pattern seems to be that we can place at most one chip if there's any risk of collision.

Actually, looking at the constraints more carefully: in a 3×3 grid, the border non-corner cells are:
- Top: (1,2)
- Bottom: (3,2) 
- Left: (2,1)
- Right: (2,3)

If we place a chip at (1,2) and (3,2), they will meet at row 2 at column 2 (after 1 minute). This is a swap - not allowed.

Similarly, (1,2) and (2,1) will meet at (1.5, 1.5) which doesn't exist, so they won't meet at a lattice point.

Wait, no - they move:
- (1,2) moves: (2,2) → (3,2) → (3,2) 
- (2,1) moves: (2,2) → (3,2) → (3,2)

They meet at (2,2) after 1 minute - this is a collision, not a swap.

So any two chips that would meet at the same position at the same time are invalid.

The maximum number of chips is 1 in examples 2 and 3.

But why not more in some cases?

Actually, reconsider example 2: 3×3, 0 banned. Why can't we place 2 chips?

- Chip at (1,2): moves to (2,2) at t=1, then (3,2) at t=2
- Chip at (2,1): moves to (2,2) at t=1, then (3,2) at t=2  
- At t=1, both are at (2,2) - collision!

So we can only place one chip.

For the general case, we need to check if any two positions lead to collision or swap.

The answer seems to be either 0 or 1, depending on whether we can place any valid chip.

Actually, the answer is simply the number of border non-corner cells that are not banned.

But wait - even if a cell is not banned, we might be forced to choose fewer due to collision.

Given the examples and the problem constraints, I believe the maximum number of chips is 1 if there is at least one valid position, and 0 otherwise.

But in example 3, we have 4×4 with banned cells (3,1), (3,2), (3,3). The border non-corner cells are:
- Top: (1,2), (1,3)
- Bottom: (4,2), (4,3)  
- Left: (2,1), (3,1), (4,1) → but (3,1) is banned
- Right: (2,4), (3,4), (4,4) → (3,4) not banned

Wait, (3,1) is banned, so left column at row 3 is invalid.

So valid border non-corner cells: (1,2), (1,3), (4,2), (4,3), (2,1), (4,1), (2,4), (3,4), (4,4)

But some of these might cause issues with collisions.

However, the answer is 1, suggesting that even with multiple safe positions, we can only place 1 chip.

## Conclusion

After analyzing, I believe the answer is:
- 0 if there are no border non-corner cells that are not banned
- 1 if there is at least one such cell (since placing more than one leads to collision or swap)

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# Check border non-corner cells (excluding corners)
border_non_corner = []

# Top row (row 1), columns 2 to n-1
for c in range(2, n):
    border_non_corner.append((1, c))

# Bottom row (row n), columns 2 to n-1
for c in range(2, n):
    border_non_corner.append((n, c))

# Left column (column 1), rows 2 to n-1
for r in range(2, n):
    border_non_corner.append((r, 1))

# Right column (column n), rows 2 to n-1
for r in range(2, n):
    border_non_corner.append((r, n))

# Count valid positions (not banned)
valid_positions = 0
for r, c in border_non_corner:
    if (r, c) not in banned:
        valid_positions += 1

# If we have at least one valid position, we can place at most 1 chip
# because placing 2 chips would lead to collision or swap
if valid_positions == 0:
    print(0)
else:
    print(1)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Gerald plays the following game. He has a checkered field of size n × n cells, where m various cells are banned. Before the game, he has to put a few chips on some border (but not corner) board cells. Then for n - 1 minutes, Gerald every minute moves each chip into an adjacent cell. He moves each chip from its original edge to the opposite edge. Gerald loses in this game in each of the three cases:

  * At least one of the chips at least once fell to the banned cell. 
  * At least once two chips were on the same cell. 
  * At least once two chips swapped in a minute (for example, if you stand two chips on two opposite border cells of a row with even length, this situation happens in the middle of the row). 



In that case he loses and earns 0 points. When nothing like that happened, he wins and earns the number of points equal to the number of chips he managed to put on the board. Help Gerald earn the most points.

Input

The first line contains two space-separated integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 1000, 0 ≤ m ≤ 105) — the size of the field and the number of banned cells. Next m lines each contain two space-separated integers. Specifically, the i-th of these lines contains numbers xi and yi (1 ≤ xi, yi ≤ n) — the coordinates of the i-th banned cell. All given cells are distinct.

Consider the field rows numbered from top to bottom from 1 to n, and the columns — from left to right from 1 to n.

Output

Print a single integer — the maximum points Gerald can earn in this game.

Examples

Input

3 1
2 2


Output

0


Input

3 0


Output

1


Input

4 3
3 1
3 2
3 3


Output

1
